People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’m losing hours to rechecking things—my dressing supplies, door locks, file dimensions, revision notes—because I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety drops.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… kind of losing a lot of time checking things. Like locks, work files, notes, even supplies when I’m getting dressed. I keep going back over them, and I don’t really feel like I can move on until I’ve done it enough that I calm down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually this thought that I missed something small. Like even if I cleaned a wound area, or checked a CAD file, it keeps coming back that maybe I missed one tiny thing… and then it could turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not always a number, but I have to go through the same steps again—like the locks, or file dimensions, or my dressing supplies—until it feels like I can stop. Sometimes that takes… a long time. Hours, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like if I don’t check the supplies, or the locks, or the file details again, something could go wrong. I keep repeating the same routine until the anxiety drops enough that I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s how it feels. Like if I don’t recheck the dressing supplies, the locks, or the file dimensions and notes, then something bad could happen and it would be on me. I end up losing hours because I don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through it enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nDoctor: Do you get a specific mental picture of something bad happening when you think about not checking these things?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not always like a clear picture, but… I’ll imagine I missed some tiny thing. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you do them have to be perfect in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… perfect in the sense that I have to do it the “right” way. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… kind of losing a lot of time checking things. Like locks, work files, notes, even supplies when I’m getting dressed. I keep going back over them, and I don’t really feel like I can move on until I’ve done it enough that I calm down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually this thought that I missed something small. Like even if I cleaned a wound area, or checked a CAD file, it keeps coming back that maybe I missed one tiny thing… and then it could turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not always a number, but I have to go through the same steps again—like the locks, or file dimensions, or my dressing supplies—until it feels like I can stop. Sometimes that takes… a long time. Hours, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like if I don’t check the supplies, or the locks, or the file details again, something could go wrong. I keep repeating the same routine until the anxiety drops enough that I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s how it feels. Like if I don’t recheck the dressing supplies, the locks, or the file dimensions and notes, then something bad could happen and it would be on me. I end up losing hours because I don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through it enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nDoctor: Do you get a specific mental picture of something bad happening when you think about not checking these things?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not always like a clear picture, but… I’ll imagine I missed some tiny thing. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them have to be done in a very precise manner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty precise. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not always a number, but I have to go through the same steps again—like the locks, or file dimensions, or my dressing supplies—until it feels like I can stop. Sometimes that takes… a long time. Hours, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like if I don’t check the supplies, or the locks, or the file details again, something could go wrong. I keep repeating the same routine until the anxiety drops enough that I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s how it feels. Like if I don’t recheck the dressing supplies, the locks, or the file dimensions and notes, then something bad could happen and it would be on me. I end up losing hours because I don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through it enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nDoctor: Do you get a specific mental picture of something bad happening when you think about not checking these things?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not always like a clear picture, but… I’ll imagine I missed some tiny thing. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you do them have to be perfect in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… perfect in the sense that I have to do it the “right” way. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them have to be done in a very precise manner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty precise. It’s not about arranging things neatly, exactly. It’s more that I have to go through the checks the same way—supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes—until it feels like I’ve done it enough. Otherwise I can’t really move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are motivated by a desire to make things symmetrical or balanced in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetry or balance, no. It’s more… I keep rechecking things, like supplies or locks or file details, until I feel like I’ve done the routine enough and the anxiety comes down. Otherwise I just don’t feel allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things exact?\n\nPatient: Yeah… exact in the sense that I have to do the checks the same way. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the routine enough times, I don’t feel like I can move on. It can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is mostly to prevent mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah… I guess it feels like if I don’t recheck the locks, the supplies, the file dimensions or notes, something might be wrong. But it’s also just that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times and the anxiety drops.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… kind of losing a lot of time checking things. Like locks, work files, notes, even supplies when I’m getting dressed. I keep going back over them, and I don’t really feel like I can move on until I’ve done it enough that I calm down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually this thought that I missed something small. Like even if I cleaned a wound area, or checked a CAD file, it keeps coming back that maybe I missed one tiny thing… and then it could turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not always a number, but I have to go through the same steps again—like the locks, or file dimensions, or my dressing supplies—until it feels like I can stop. Sometimes that takes… a long time. Hours, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like if I don’t check the supplies, or the locks, or the file details again, something could go wrong. I keep repeating the same routine until the anxiety drops enough that I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s how it feels. Like if I don’t recheck the dressing supplies, the locks, or the file dimensions and notes, then something bad could happen and it would be on me. I end up losing hours because I don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through it enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nDoctor: Do you get a specific mental picture of something bad happening when you think about not checking these things?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not always like a clear picture, but… I’ll imagine I missed some tiny thing. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you do them have to be perfect in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… perfect in the sense that I have to do it the “right” way. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them have to be done in a very precise manner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty precise. It’s not about arranging things neatly, exactly. It’s more that I have to go through the checks the same way—supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes—until it feels like I’ve done it enough. Otherwise I can’t really move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are motivated by a desire to make things symmetrical or balanced in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetry or balance, no. It’s more… I keep rechecking things, like supplies or locks or file details, until I feel like I’ve done the routine enough and the anxiety comes down. Otherwise I just don’t feel allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things exact?\n\nPatient: Yeah… exact in the sense that I have to do the checks the same way. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the routine enough times, I don’t feel like I can move on. It can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is mostly to prevent mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah… I guess it feels like if I don’t recheck the locks, the supplies, the file dimensions or notes, something might be wrong. But it’s also just that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times and the anxiety drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to control something when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m trying to get control over whether I can move on or not. I keep rechecking the supplies, locks, file details… until the anxiety drops enough. It’s not really satisfying, it just eats up hours.\n\nDoctor: Is your main fear that you will make a mistake if you don't check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the main fear. Even when I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed one small thing — like in a CAD file, or even cleaning a wound — and that it could turn into something serious, like someone getting hurt. I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I keep going back over them, and I don’t really feel like I can move on until I’ve done it enough that I calm down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and are difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s usually this thought that I missed something small. Like even if I cleaned a wound area, or checked a CAD file, it keeps coming back that maybe I missed one tiny thing… and then it could turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I’ve already checked, but it doesn’t really go away when I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not always a number, but I have to go through the same steps again—like the locks, or file dimensions, or my dressing supplies—until it feels like I can stop. Sometimes that takes… a long time. Hours, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like if I don’t check the supplies, or the locks, or the file details again, something could go wrong. I keep repeating the same routine until the anxiety drops enough that I feel like I’m allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are necessary to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s how it feels. Like if I don’t recheck the dressing supplies, the locks, or the file dimensions and notes, then something bad could happen and it would be on me. I end up losing hours because I don’t feel like I can move on until I’ve gone through it enough times and the anxiety comes down.\n\nDoctor: Do you get a specific mental picture of something bad happening when you think about not checking these things?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not always like a clear picture, but… I’ll imagine I missed some tiny thing. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you do them have to be perfect in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… perfect in the sense that I have to do it the “right” way. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them have to be done in a very precise manner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty precise. It’s not about arranging things neatly, exactly. It’s more that I have to go through the checks the same way—supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes—until it feels like I’ve done it enough. Otherwise I can’t really move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are motivated by a desire to make things symmetrical or balanced in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetry or balance, no. It’s more… I keep rechecking things, like supplies or locks or file details, until I feel like I’ve done the routine enough and the anxiety comes down. Otherwise I just don’t feel allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things exact?\n\nPatient: Yeah… exact in the sense that I have to do the checks the same way. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the routine enough times, I don’t feel like I can move on. It can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is mostly to prevent mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah… I guess it feels like if I don’t recheck the locks, the supplies, the file dimensions or notes, something might be wrong. But it’s also just that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times and the anxiety drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to control something when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m trying to get control over whether I can move on or not. I keep rechecking the supplies, locks, file details… until the anxiety drops enough. It’s not really satisfying, it just eats up hours.\n\nDoctor: Is your main fear that you will make a mistake if you don't check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the main fear. Even when I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed one small thing — like in a CAD file, or even cleaning a wound — and that it could turn into something serious, like someone getting hurt. I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific thought or image that triggers your need to recheck things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s usually this thought that I missed one tiny thing. Like even after I cleaned around a wound, or checked a CAD file, I’ll suddenly think, what if I overlooked something and it turns into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I already checked, but it doesn’t really… go away just because I tell myself that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like the wound area wasn’t really clean enough and it gets infected, or I overlooked something in a CAD file and it turns into a mistake that hurts someone. Even when I know I checked, the thought just… cuts back in, and I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sort of. It’s not so much that things have to look perfect, but I have to go through the same checking routine. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes… if I don’t do it in the right sequence or enough times, I don’t feel okay moving on. I can lose hours that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them need to be symmetrical or exact in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s more… exact in the sense that I have to recheck the supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes, in the same way enough times before I feel like I can stop. If I don’t, I just feel stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you do them have to be perfect in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… perfect in the sense that I have to do it the “right” way. Like checking the dressing stuff, the locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the same routine enough times, I don’t feel like I’m allowed to move on. I can lose a lot of time to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks are helping to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It feels like if I don’t recheck the supplies or the locks or the files the right number of times, something could be wrong and I’d be responsible. Mostly I keep doing it until the anxiety drops enough that I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or the way you arrange them have to be done in a very precise manner?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty precise. It’s not about arranging things neatly, exactly. It’s more that I have to go through the checks the same way—supplies, locks, file dimensions, notes—until it feels like I’ve done it enough. Otherwise I can’t really move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are motivated by a desire to make things symmetrical or balanced in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetry or balance, no. It’s more… I keep rechecking things, like supplies or locks or file details, until I feel like I’ve done the routine enough and the anxiety comes down. Otherwise I just don’t feel allowed to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things exact?\n\nPatient: Yeah… exact in the sense that I have to do the checks the same way. Like supplies, locks, file dimensions, revision notes. If I don’t go through the routine enough times, I don’t feel like I can move on. It can take hours sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is mostly to prevent mistakes from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah… I guess it feels like if I don’t recheck the locks, the supplies, the file dimensions or notes, something might be wrong. But it’s also just that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve done the routine enough times and the anxiety drops.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to control something when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like I’m trying to get control over whether I can move on or not. I keep rechecking the supplies, locks, file details… until the anxiety drops enough. It’s not really satisfying, it just eats up hours.\n\nDoctor: Is your main fear that you will make a mistake if you don't check things repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the main fear. Even when I know I checked it, I get this thought that I missed one small thing — like in a CAD file, or even cleaning a wound — and that it could turn into something serious, like someone getting hurt. I can’t really talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific thought or image that triggers your need to recheck things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s usually this thought that I missed one tiny thing. Like even after I cleaned around a wound, or checked a CAD file, I’ll suddenly think, what if I overlooked something and it turns into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone. I know I already checked, but it doesn’t really… go away just because I tell myself that.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to recheck things more about preventing a mistake or about feeling in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly preventing a mistake, I guess… but it also feels like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough. Like with supplies, locks, dimensions, revision notes — I keep going back through the same routine until the anxiety drops a bit. It can take hours.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
